    \makeatletter
    \newsavebox\pandoc@box
    \newcommand*\pandocbounded[1]{%
      \sbox\pandoc@box{#1}%
      % scaling factors for width and height
      \Gscale@div\@tempa\textheight{\dimexpr\ht\pandoc@box+\dp\pandoc@box\relax}%
      \Gscale@div\@tempb\linewidth{\wd\pandoc@box}%
      % select the smaller of both
      \ifdim\@tempb\p@<\@tempa\p@
        \let\@tempa\@tempb
      \fi
      % scaling accordingly (\@tempa < 1)
      \ifdim\@tempa\p@<\p@
        \scalebox{\@tempa}{\usebox\pandoc@box}%
      % scaling not needed, use as it is
      \else
        \usebox{\pandoc@box}%
      \fi
    }
    \makeatother

\def\PY@reset{\let\PY@it=\relax \let\PY@bf=\relax%
    \let\PY@ul=\relax \let\PY@tc=\relax%
    \let\PY@bc=\relax \let\PY@ff=\relax}
\def\PY@tok#1{\csname PY@tok@#1\endcsname}
\def\PY@toks#1+{\ifx\relax#1\empty\else%
    \PY@tok{#1}\expandafter\PY@toks\fi}
\def\PY@do#1{\PY@bc{\PY@tc{\PY@ul{%
    \PY@it{\PY@bf{\PY@ff{#1}}}}}}}
\def\PY#1#2{\PY@reset\PY@toks#1+\relax+\PY@do{#2}}

    % For linebreaks inside Verbatim environment from package fancyvrb.
    \makeatletter
        \newbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox
        \newbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox
        \newcommand*\Wrappedvisiblespace {\textcolor{red}{\textvisiblespace}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol {\textcolor{red}{\llap{\tiny$\m@th\hookrightarrow$}}}
        \newcommand*\Wrappedcontinuationindent {3ex }
        \newcommand*\Wrappedafterbreak {\kern\Wrappedcontinuationindent\copy\Wrappedcontinuationbox}
        % Take advantage of the already applied Pygments mark-up to insert
        % potential linebreaks for TeX processing.
        %        {, <, #, %, $, ' and ": go to next line.
        %        _, }, ^, &, >, - and ~: stay at end of broken line.
        % Use of \textquotesingle for straight quote.
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatspecials {%
            \def\PYGZus{\discretionary{\char`\_}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\_}}%
            \def\PYGZob{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\{}{\char`\{}}%
            \def\PYGZcb{\discretionary{\char`\}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\}}}%
            \def\PYGZca{\discretionary{\char`\^}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\^}}%
            \def\PYGZam{\discretionary{\char`\&}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\&}}%
            \def\PYGZlt{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\<}{\char`\<}}%
            \def\PYGZgt{\discretionary{\char`\>}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\>}}%
            \def\PYGZsh{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\#}{\char`\#}}%
            \def\PYGZpc{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\%}{\char`\%}}%
            \def\PYGZdl{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\$}{\char`\$}}%
            \def\PYGZhy{\discretionary{\char`\-}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\-}}%
            \def\PYGZsq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\textquotesingle}{\textquotesingle}}%
            \def\PYGZdq{\discretionary{}{\Wrappedafterbreak\char`\"}{\char`\"}}%
            \def\PYGZti{\discretionary{\char`\~}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\char`\~}}%
        }
        % Some characters . , ; ? ! / are not pygmentized.
        % This macro makes them "active" and they will insert potential linebreaks
        \newcommand*\Wrappedbreaksatpunct {%
            \lccode`\~`\.\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\.}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\.}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\,\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\,}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\,}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\;\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\;}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\;}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\:\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\:}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\:}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\?\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\?}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\?}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\!\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\!}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\!}}}%
            \lccode`\~`\/\lowercase{\def~}{\discretionary{\hbox{\char`\/}}{\Wrappedafterbreak}{\hbox{\char`\/}}}%
            \catcode`\.\active
            \catcode`\,\active
            \catcode`\;\active
            \catcode`\:\active
            \catcode`\?\active
            \catcode`\!\active
            \catcode`\/\active
            \lccode`\~`\~
        }
    \makeatother

    \let\OriginalVerbatim=\Verbatim
    \makeatletter
    \renewcommand{\Verbatim}[1][1]{%
        %\parskip\z@skip
        \sbox\Wrappedcontinuationbox {\Wrappedcontinuationsymbol}%
        \sbox\Wrappedvisiblespacebox {\FV@SetupFont\Wrappedvisiblespace}%
        \def\FancyVerbFormatLine ##1{\hsize\linewidth
            \vtop{\raggedright\hyphenpenalty\z@\exhyphenpenalty\z@
                \doublehyphendemerits\z@\finalhyphendemerits\z@
                \strut ##1\strut}%
        }%
        % If the linebreak is at a space, the latter will be displayed as visible
        % space at end of first line, and a continuation symbol starts next line.
        % Stretch/shrink are however usually zero for typewriter font.
        \def\FV@Space {%
            \nobreak\hskip\z@ plus\fontdimen3\font minus\fontdimen4\font
            \discretionary{\copy\Wrappedvisiblespacebox}{\Wrappedafterbreak}
            {\kern\fontdimen2\font}%
        }%

        % Allow breaks at special characters using \PYG... macros.
        \Wrappedbreaksatspecials
        % Breaks at punctuation characters . , ; ? ! and / need catcode=\active
        \OriginalVerbatim[#1,codes*=\Wrappedbreaksatpunct]%
    }
    \makeatother

    \#\textbf{Pillar 5: Capstone Project: Early Warning Student Dropout Risk
Prediction System} \#\#\#\# Submitted by: \textbf{Rex Villamar}
\#\#\#\#\# \emph{24 September 2026}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\textbf{Selected Domain:} Education (Predict Student Dropout Risk)

\textbf{Dataset:} \emph{Predict Students' Dropout and Academic Success}
(UCI Machine Learning Repository; Realinho et al., 2022), \(N = 4,424\)
students, 36 features

\textbf{Target Environment:} Google Colab / Jupyter Notebook

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{the-story-in-one-slide}{%
\subsubsection{The Story in One Slide}\label{the-story-in-one-slide}}

\textbf{Problem.} Student dropout costs institutions tuition revenue and
costs students their qualification. This notebook builds a classifier
that flags, \textbf{at the end of Semester 2}, the students most likely
to drop out, so advisors can act while later semesters remain.

\textbf{Approach.} Every design decision is made to serve one operating
constraint: \emph{missing a future dropout is far more costly than an
unnecessary advisory check-in}, so the system is tuned and judged on
\textbf{recall} (target \(\ge 0.85\)), with precision tracked as the
price.

1 & What are we predicting, and how is success judged? & Binary dropout
target, decision point at end of Semester 2, recall target and F2
selection metric \\
2 & What data do we have? & UCI dataset, quality audit, data
dictionary \\
3 & How does raw data become model input? & Cleaning audit, four
engineered features, sensitive attributes withheld, RFECV selection, PCA
diagnostics \\
4 & Which model is best? & Four model families tuned by 5-fold CV on F2;
selection on training CV only \\
5 & Can the model be trusted and explained? & Leakage and overfitting
audit, SHAP and LIME, fairness audit by group, evaluated bias
mitigation, findings \\
6-7 & How is it communicated and shipped? & Slide export for two
audiences, repository structure \\
\end{longtable}

    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{step-1-problem-understanding-framing}{%
\subsection{Step 1: Problem Understanding \&
Framing}\label{step-1-problem-understanding-framing}}

\hypertarget{business-context-problem-statement}{%
\subsubsection{1.1 Business Context \& Problem
Statement}\label{business-context-problem-statement}}

\begin{quote}
Higher education institutions face significant revenue loss and reduced
institutional ranking due to unexpected student dropouts. Acquiring a
new student costs significantly more than retaining an existing student.
By detecting dropout indicators early (at the end of Semester 2, once
both semesters' academic records are available), academic advisory teams
can deliver targeted interventions---such as tutoring, counseling, or
financial aid updates---before a student officially drops out.
\end{quote}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{data-science-task-framing-target-definition}{%
\subsubsection{1.2 Data Science Task Framing \& Target
Definition}\label{data-science-task-framing-target-definition}}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Task Classification:} Supervised Binary Classification.
\item
  \textbf{Decision Point:} End of Semester 2. Each student is scored
  once their 1st- and 2nd-semester records are complete, leaving later
  semesters in which an intervention can still change the outcome. Every
  feature used is available by this point.
\item
  \textbf{Feature Vector (\(X\)):} Demographic background (excluding the
  sensitive attributes below), socio-economic factors, application
  metadata, and 1st/2nd-semester academic performance.
\item
  \textbf{Responsible-AI Constraint:} \texttt{Gender} and
  \texttt{Marital\ status} are \textbf{not} model inputs; they are kept
  only to audit whether outcomes differ across groups.
\item
  \textbf{Target Mapping (\(y\)):}
  \[\text{Target } (y) = \begin{cases} 1 & \text{if Status} = \text{'Dropout' (High Risk)} \\ 0 & \text{if Status} \in \{\text{'Graduate'}, \text{'Enrolled'}\} \text{ (Retained)} \end{cases}\]
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{asymmetric-cost-matrix-evaluation-metrics}{%
\subsubsection{1.3 Asymmetric Cost Matrix \& Evaluation
Metrics}\label{asymmetric-cost-matrix-evaluation-metrics}}

\[\begin{array}{c||c|c} \text{Actual / Predicted} & \text{Predicted High Risk } (\hat{y}=1) & \text{Predicted Retained } (\hat{y}=0) \\ \hline \text{Actual Dropout } (y=1) & \text{True Positive (TP) — Successful Intervention} & \mathbf{False\ Negative\ (FN)\ —\ High\ Cost\ (Lost\ Student)} \\ \hline \text{Actual Retained } (y=0) & \mathbf{False\ Positive\ (FP)\ —\ Low\ Cost\ (Advisory\ Check-in)} & \text{True Negative (TN) — Baseline Operational State} \end{array}\]

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Primary Technical Metric:} \textbf{Recall (Sensitivity)} on
  Class 1 (\(\frac{TP}{TP + FN}\)), targeting \(\ge 85\%\) detection
  rate for actual dropouts.
\item
  \textbf{Secondary Technical Metrics:} \textbf{F2-Score} (emphasizing
  Recall twice as much as Precision), \textbf{ROC-AUC}, and
  \textbf{PR-AUC}.
\item
  \textbf{Evaluation Protocol:} Stratified \(80/20\) train/test split
  (\texttt{random\_state=42}). Models are tuned and \textbf{selected on
  training-set cross-validation} (\(F_2\)); the held-out test set only
  \emph{reports} final performance at a fixed decision threshold of
  \(0.50\). Deployment criterion: Recall \(\ge 85\%\), with Precision
  tracked as the cost of that recall.
\item
  \textbf{Business KPIs:}
\end{itemize}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Student Retention Rate Increase (\%):} Year-over-year
  improvement in retained students.
\item
  \textbf{Net Saved Tuition Revenue (\$):}
  \((\text{Retained Students} \times \text{Annual Tuition}) - \text{Intervention Operating Cost}.\)
\end{enumerate}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{How the business KPIs are evaluated:} the executive deck
  converts the measured recall and precision into students retained,
  tuition revenue preserved and advisory workload (Step 6).
\end{itemize}

    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{step-2-data-collection-understanding}{%
\subsection{Step 2: Data Collection \&
Understanding}\label{step-2-data-collection-understanding}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Applied Concept:} Automated Ingestion Pipeline, Data Quality
  Auditing, and Feature Dictionary Profiling.
\item
  \textbf{Source and justification:} \emph{Predict Students' Dropout and
  Academic Success} (UCI repository; Realinho et al., 2022) links
  enrolment records with first- and second-semester academic results for
  \(N = 4,424\) students and 36 features, and labels each student
  \emph{Dropout}, \emph{Enrolled} or \emph{Graduate}. It is a public,
  documented, real institutional dataset whose columns match the
  decision we want to support (who is at risk after two semesters).
\item
  \textbf{How It Works:}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Ingestion and quality audit:} The zip is downloaded
    programmatically, parsed (\texttt{;} delimited) and column names are
    stripped of stray whitespace. Missing-value and duplicate counts are
    checked before any modelling.
  \item
    \textbf{Class ratio:} Graduate \(49.93\%\), Dropout \(32.12\%\),
    Enrolled \(17.95\%\). Collapsing \emph{Graduate} and \emph{Enrolled}
    into \emph{Retained} (Step 3) yields a binary target with a \(32\%\)
    positive class.
  \item
    \textbf{Data dictionary:} Every column is assigned to one of four
    domain groups plus the target, with its data type and range or
    cardinality:

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      \emph{Demographic:} marital status, nationality, gender, age at
      enrolment, international, displaced, special educational needs.
    \item
      \emph{Socio-Economic:} parents' qualifications and occupations,
      debtor, scholarship holder, tuition fees up to date.
    \item
      \emph{Application and Macroeconomic:} application mode and order,
      course, attendance mode, previous qualification and grade,
      admission grade, unemployment, inflation, GDP.
    \item
      \emph{Academic (Semester 1 and 2):} credited, enrolled, evaluated,
      approved units, grades and units without evaluation, per semester.
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    \textbf{Encoding caveat:} categorical variables arrive
    integer-coded; their code meanings are in the UCI documentation, and
    the dictionary reports each one's cardinality.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

    \textbf{Step 2 takeaways (computed from the data)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Size and quality:} 4,424 students, 36 features plus the
  3-class \texttt{Target}; 0 missing values and 0 duplicate rows, so no
  imputation or de-duplication is required.
\item
  \textbf{Class balance:} Graduate 49.9\%, Dropout 32.1\%, Enrolled
  17.9\%. Collapsing Graduate + Enrolled into \emph{Retained} gives a
  binary problem with a 32.1\% positive class, i.e.~moderate imbalance
  that must be handled in modelling and in metric choice.
\item
  \textbf{Feature groups:} Academic (Sem 1 \& 2): 12, Application/Macro:
  10, Demographic: 7, Socio-Economic: 7.
\item
  \textbf{Encoding:} categorical variables (course, application mode,
  qualifications, occupations, nationality, etc.) are already
  integer-coded in the source file; the dictionary reports their
  cardinality, while code meanings are given in the UCI documentation.
\end{itemize}

    
    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{step-3-data-preprocessing-eda-feature-engineering}{%
\subsection{Step 3: Data Preprocessing, EDA \& Feature
Engineering}\label{step-3-data-preprocessing-eda-feature-engineering}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Applied Concept:} Target Binarization, Data-Quality and
  Outlier Audit, Domain Feature Synthesis, Applied EDA,
  Sensitive-Attribute Policy, RFECV Selection, and PCA Diagnostics.
\item
  \textbf{How It Works:}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Target mapping:} \(y = 1\) for \emph{Dropout} and \(y = 0\)
    for \emph{Graduate} or \emph{Enrolled}.
  \item
    \textbf{Cleaning audit:} Nulls and duplicates are counted. Outliers
    are screened with the \(1.5 \times \text{IQR}\) rule on grades,
    ages, unit counts and macro variables; flagged values are
    \textbf{kept}, because they are genuine observations, tree ensembles
    are insensitive to them, and Logistic Regression standardises its
    inputs inside its pipeline.
  \item
    \textbf{Domain features} (computed row-wise, so they cannot leak
    across the split):

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      \emph{Approval rate:}
      \(\text{Approval\_Rate}_s = \frac{\text{Units Approved}_s}{\text{Units Enrolled}_s + 10^{-5}}\)
      for each semester \(s\).
    \item
      \emph{Academic momentum:}
      \(\text{Grade\_Trend} = \text{Grade}_{\text{Sem2}} - \text{Grade}_{\text{Sem1}}\).
    \item
      \emph{Financial stress:}
      \(\text{Debtor} + (1 - \text{Tuition Up To Date}) + (1 - \text{Scholarship Holder})\).
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    \textbf{Applied EDA:} three views test whether these ideas carry
    signal: a boxplot of \texttt{Grade\_Trend} by outcome, dropout
    counts by \texttt{Financial\_Stress\_Index}, and the ten features
    most correlated with the target.
  \item
    \textbf{Sensitive-attribute policy:} \texttt{Gender} and
    \texttt{Marital\ status} are removed from the model inputs and kept
    in a separate audit table, used only in Step 5 to measure whether
    outcomes differ by group.
  \item
    \textbf{Split-then-fit protocol:} a stratified \(80/20\) split
    (\texttt{random\_state=42}) is made first; the
    \texttt{StandardScaler}, RFECV and PCA are then fitted on
    \textbf{training data only}, so no test information influences
    preprocessing.
  \item
    \textbf{Feature selection (wrapper):} RFECV with a Random Forest and
    recall scoring removes features that do not help cross-validated
    recall; the selected set is what Step 4 trains on.
  \item
    \textbf{Dimensionality reduction:} PCA retaining \(95\%\) of the
    variance is used to diagnose redundancy and to visualise class
    overlap in two dimensions. It is \textbf{not} fed to the models,
    because principal components would make SHAP explanations in Step 5
    uninterpretable.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

    \begin{center}
    \adjustimage{max size={0.9\linewidth}{0.9\paperheight}}{Technical Slides_Rex Villamar_Pillar 5 Capstone Project_files/Technical Slides_Rex Villamar_Pillar 5 Capstone Project_5_3.png}
    \end{center}
    { \hspace*{\fill} \\}
    
    \textbf{Step 3 takeaways (computed from the data)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Cleaning:} 0 missing values and 0 duplicates, so nothing was
  imputed or dropped. 9 of 12 continuous / count features contain
  IQR-rule outliers (largest:
  \texttt{Curricular\ units\ 2nd\ sem\ (grade)}, 19.82\% of rows). These
  are genuine observations (e.g.~mature students, many credited units),
  so they are \textbf{retained}; the tree ensembles are insensitive to
  them and Logistic Regression standardises its inputs inside its
  pipeline.
\item
  \textbf{Split:} 3,539 training / 885 test students, stratified
  (dropout rate 32.1\% train, 32.1\% test). Scaler, RFECV and PCA are
  fitted on training data only.
\item
  \textbf{Sensitive attributes:} \texttt{Gender} and
  \texttt{Marital\ status} are excluded from the model inputs and kept
  in \texttt{audit\_train} / \texttt{audit\_test} for the Step 5
  fairness audit.
\item
  \textbf{EDA:} median \texttt{Grade\_Trend} is +0.00 for retained vs
  +0.00 for dropout students; dropout rate by
  \texttt{Financial\_Stress\_Index} level is 0: 9.3\%, 1: 29.3\%, 2:
  69.2\%, 3: 88.8\%.
\item
  \textbf{Feature selection:} RFECV (recall-scored) kept 38 of 38
  features (it dropped none, so every feature contributed to
  cross-validated recall). The selected set is what Step 4 trains on.
\item
  \textbf{Dimensionality reduction:} 24 principal components are needed
  for 95\% of the variance of 38 features, so the information is spread
  across many directions rather than a few; the PC1-PC2 projection shows
  the classes overlapping heavily, i.e.~no clean linear separation. PCA
  is therefore used for diagnosis and visualisation only; models keep
  the original features so SHAP explanations stay interpretable.
\end{itemize}

    
    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{step-4-model-implementation-comparison}{%
\subsection{Step 4: Model Implementation \&
Comparison}\label{step-4-model-implementation-comparison}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Applied Concept:} Multi-Model Implementation, Class-Imbalance
  Handling, Stratified Cross-Validation, Hyperparameter Optimisation,
  Leakage-Free Model Selection, and Artefact Serialisation.
\item
  \textbf{How It Works:}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Model suite} (increasing capacity):

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      \emph{Logistic Regression:} linear baseline; a
      \texttt{StandardScaler} sits inside its pipeline so scaling is
      re-fitted on every CV training fold.
    \item
      \emph{Random Forest:} bagged decision trees that reduce variance.
    \item
      \emph{XGBoost and LightGBM:} gradient-boosted trees that
      sequentially correct earlier errors; they can capture non-linear
      interactions such as approval rate combined with financial stress.
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    \textbf{Class asymmetry:} with only \(32\%\) dropouts, grids include
    class weighting (\texttt{class\_weight} for scikit-learn and
    LightGBM, \texttt{scale\_pos\_weight} for XGBoost) so the minority
    class counts more in the loss.
  \item
    \textbf{Tuning objective:} \(F_2 = 5PR / (4P + R)\),
    i.e.~\(F_\beta\) with \(\beta = 2\), which weights recall twice as
    heavily as precision to mirror the cost matrix of Step 1.
  \item
    \textbf{Selection without leakage:} each model is tuned by
    \texttt{GridSearchCV} with stratified 5-fold CV on the
    \textbf{training set}, and models are ranked by cross-validated
    \(F_2\). The hold-out set is used only to \emph{report} performance
    at a fixed threshold of \(0.50\); it plays no part in choosing the
    model.
  \item
    \textbf{Reported metrics:} recall, precision, \(F_2\), ROC-AUC and
    PR-AUC. The fold standard deviation of the winner is shown so the
    ranking margin can be judged against noise.
  \item
    \textbf{Reproducibility:} every tuned model (\texttt{.joblib}), the
    comparison table (\texttt{.csv}) and the final configuration
    (\texttt{.json}: parameters, threshold, seed, split, feature list,
    excluded attributes) are written to \texttt{models/}.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\prompt{In}{incolor}{3}{\boxspacing}

    \textbf{Step 4 takeaways (computed from the results)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Selected model:} \textbf{XGBoost}, ranked first on
  cross-validated F2 (0.8247 +/- 0.0199); the test set played no part in
  the choice.
\item
  \textbf{Hold-out performance:} recall 0.912, precision 0.685, F2
  0.855, ROC-AUC 0.937, PR-AUC 0.911. The recall target of 0.85 is
  \textbf{met}, and the precision of 0.685 shows the advisory workload
  that recall costs (the rest of the flags are check-ins that turn out
  to be unnecessary).
\item
  \textbf{Margin over the runner-up (Logistic Regression):} 0.0191
  CV-F2, which is smaller than one fold standard deviation (0.0199), so
  the ranking is not statistically distinguishable; the choice among the
  top models is a judgement call.
\item
  \textbf{Reproducibility:} all tuned models, the comparison table and
  the final configuration (parameters, threshold, seed, feature list)
  are saved in \texttt{models/}.
\end{itemize}

    
    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{step-5a-is-the-model-strong-leakage-free-and-generalising}{%
\subsection{Step 5a: Is the Model Strong, Leakage-Free and
Generalising?}\label{step-5a-is-the-model-strong-leakage-free-and-generalising}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Applied Concept:} Final Hold-Out Evaluation, Feature-Timing
  and Leakage Audit, Overfitting and Imbalance Diagnostics.
\item
  \textbf{Audit target:} the model selected in Step 4, on the same
  split, at the same threshold (\(0.50\)), so every number here is
  directly comparable with the Step 4 table.
\item
  \textbf{How It Works:}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Final performance:} confusion matrix and precision-recall
    curve show the operating point against the recall target of \(0.85\)
    defined in Step 1.
  \item
    \textbf{Feature timing:} the system decides at the end of Semester
    2, so Semester 1 and 2 records are legitimate inputs. A timing table
    confirms every feature exists by then, and a with/without-Semester-2
    comparison quantifies what the second semester adds.
  \item
    \textbf{Leakage screen:} each feature's standalone ROC-AUC is
    computed (\(\max(\text{AUC}, 1-\text{AUC})\)); a lone feature above
    \(0.90\) would signal that it leaks the outcome.
  \item
    \textbf{Overfitting:} train versus validation scores from stratified
    5-fold CV give the generalisation gap; a gap above \(0.10\) in
    \(F_2\) is treated as overfitting and the conclusion text changes
    accordingly.
  \item
    \textbf{Imbalance:} the cost of class weighting appears as lower
    precision, and is stated rather than hidden.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

    \textbf{Step 5a takeaways (computed from the results)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Hold-out performance (XGBoost, threshold 0.5):} recall 0.912,
  precision 0.685, F2 0.855, ROC-AUC 0.937, PR-AUC 0.911. The recall
  target of 0.85 is \textbf{met}.
\item
  \textbf{Operating point:} the model flags 378 of 885 test students
  (42.7\%); 119 of those flags are false alarms (one advisory check-in
  each) and 25 actual dropouts are missed.
\item
  \textbf{Value of Semester 2 data:} ROC-AUC rises from 0.912 (Semester
  1 only) to 0.937, and recall from 0.880 to 0.912, which is the gain
  bought by waiting one more semester.
\item
  \textbf{Leakage screen:} the strongest single feature is
  \texttt{Approval\_Rate\_Sem2} with AUC 0.871; no single feature
  exceeds 0.90, so there is no evidence that any one variable leaks the
  outcome.
\item
  \textbf{Generalisation:} train-minus-validation F2 gap is 0.049, so
  the model shows an acceptable generalisation gap (\textless= 0.10).
\item
  \textbf{Class imbalance:} dropouts are 32.1\% of training rows; class
  weighting was part of every grid search, and the cost is the precision
  reported above.
\end{itemize}

    
    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{step-5b-why-does-the-model-flag-a-student-shap-lime}{%
\subsection{Step 5b: Why Does the Model Flag a Student? (SHAP \&
LIME)}\label{step-5b-why-does-the-model-flag-a-student-shap-lime}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Applied Concept:} Global and Local Explainability.
\item
  \textbf{How It Works:}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{SHAP (global):} \texttt{TreeExplainer} decomposes each
    prediction additively, \(f(x) = \phi_0 + \sum_j \phi_j(x)\), in
    log-odds of dropout. The beeswarm plot shows each feature's effect
    for every student, and mean \(|\phi_j|\) ranks the drivers.
  \item
    \textbf{LIME (local):} a sparse linear surrogate is fitted around
    one correctly flagged dropout (median risk among true positives) to
    show which feature values moved that single prediction.
  \item
    \textbf{Why both:} SHAP and LIME are independent attribution
    methods; agreement between them on the main drivers raises
    confidence that the explanation reflects the model.
  \item
    \textbf{Consistency check with Step 3:} because gender and marital
    status were withheld, they cannot appear here; the drivers should
    instead be academic and financial signals.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

    \textbf{Step 5b takeaways (computed from the results)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Global drivers:} the three largest mean
  \textbar SHAP\textbar{} features are \texttt{Approval\_Rate\_Sem2}
  (higher values push risk DOWN); \texttt{Approval\_Rate\_Sem1} (higher
  values push risk DOWN); \texttt{Tuition\ fees\ up\ to\ date} (higher
  values push risk DOWN).
\item
  \textbf{Timing:} 3 of the top 10 drivers are Semester 2 variables
  (\texttt{Approval\_Rate\_Sem2},
  \texttt{Curricular\ units\ 2nd\ sem\ (approved)},
  \texttt{Curricular\ units\ 2nd\ sem\ (grade)}); the end-of-Semester-2
  decision point is what exposes this academic signal to the model.
\item
  \textbf{Engineered features:} 3 of the top 10 are features built in
  Step 3 (\texttt{Approval\_Rate\_Sem2}, \texttt{Approval\_Rate\_Sem1},
  \texttt{Financial\_Stress\_Index}).
\item
  \textbf{Local explanation (LIME):} for student 3744 (predicted risk
  97\%), the strongest local contributions are
  \texttt{Approval\_Rate\_Sem2\ \textless{}=\ 0.40} (+0.251);
  \texttt{Financial\_Stress\_Index\ \textless{}=\ 1.00} (-0.156);
  \texttt{Nacionality\ \textless{}=\ 1.00} (+0.107). Agreement with the
  global view: 2 of the 3 LIME features matched to column names also
  rank in the SHAP top 10, so the two methods only partly agree and the
  local story should be read with care.
\item
  \textbf{Sensitive attributes:} \texttt{Gender} and
  \texttt{Marital\ status} are not model inputs, so they cannot appear
  in these attributions.
\end{itemize}

    
    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{step-5c-does-the-model-treat-groups-fairly-bias-fairness-audit}{%
\subsection{Step 5c: Does the Model Treat Groups Fairly? (Bias \&
Fairness
Audit)}\label{step-5c-does-the-model-treat-groups-fairly-bias-fairness-audit}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Applied Concept:} Subgroup Auditing with Demographic-Parity,
  Equal-Opportunity and Equalized-Odds Metrics.
\item
  \textbf{How It Works:}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Groups audited:} gender, marital status (single vs not),
    scholarship status (a socio-economic proxy), debtor status and age
    group (\(\le 20\), \(21\text{-}25\), \(> 25\)). The first two come
    from the audit table because they are not model inputs. Groups with
    fewer than \(30\) students are excluded from ratios.
  \item
    \textbf{Metrics per group:} selection rate, recall (TPR),
    false-positive rate (FPR) and precision.
  \item
    \textbf{Fairness criteria:}

    \begin{itemize}
    \tightlist
    \item
      \emph{Disparate impact} \(= \min_g SR_g / \max_g SR_g\), where
      \(SR_g\) is the share of group \(g\) flagged; values below
      \(0.80\) are flagged.
    \item
      \emph{Equal opportunity} \(= \max_g TPR_g - \min_g TPR_g\); gaps
      above \(0.10\) are flagged.
    \item
      \emph{Equalized odds} requires both the TPR and FPR gaps to be
      small.
    \end{itemize}
  \item
    \textbf{Primary criterion:} recall parity. Selection-rate gaps
    partly reflect real differences in base dropout rates, whereas
    unequal recall means one group's at-risk students are missed more
    often, which is the harm that matters here.
  \item
    \textbf{Does excluding sensitive attributes help?} A counterfactual
    model that \emph{is} given gender and marital status is trained and
    compared with the deployed model, showing both the fairness effect
    and the accuracy cost of the exclusion.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

  0.80); \textbf{Marital status} (disparate impact 0.64 \textless{}
  0.80); \textbf{Scholarship holder} (disparate impact 0.32 \textless{}
  0.80; recall gap 0.13 \textgreater{} 0.10); \textbf{Debtor} (disparate
  impact 0.52 \textless{} 0.80); \textbf{Age group} (disparate impact
  0.42 \textless{} 0.80; recall gap 0.18 \textgreater{} 0.10).
\item
  \textbf{Largest recall gap:} \textbf{Age group} at 0.180 (disparate
  impact 0.419).
\item
  \textbf{How to read disparate impact:} selection-rate ratios partly
  mirror genuine differences in dropout rate between groups (see the
  \texttt{Dropout\ rate} column), so recall parity is the primary
  criterion; the harm to avoid is a group of at-risk students being
  missed more often than others.
\item
  \textbf{Excluding sensitive attributes:} the gender recall gap is
  0.012 without them versus 0.041 when they are included, and ROC-AUC
  moves from 0.937 to 0.937. Excluding them costs little accuracy;
  because other features can act as proxies, outcomes are still audited
  by group rather than assumed fair.
\end{itemize}

    
    \begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\hypertarget{step-5d-can-the-gaps-be-reduced-bias-mitigation-evaluated}{%
\subsection{Step 5d: Can the Gaps Be Reduced? (Bias Mitigation,
Evaluated)}\label{step-5d-can-the-gaps-be-reduced-bias-mitigation-evaluated}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Applied Concept:} In-Processing and Post-Processing Mitigation
  with a Pre-Declared Decision Rule.
\item
  \textbf{How It Works:}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Target:} the attribute with the largest baseline recall gap
    in Step 5c.
  \item
    \textbf{Reweighing (in-processing):} each training row receives
    weight \(w(g, y) = \frac{P(g)\,P(y)}{P(g, y)}\), which makes group
    and outcome statistically independent in the weighted training set;
    the model is then refitted.
  \item
    \textbf{Group thresholds (post-processing):} a separate decision
    threshold per group is chosen on \textbf{out-of-fold training
    predictions} so that each group's recall matches the overall level;
    the test set is never consulted when choosing thresholds.
  \item
    \textbf{Evaluation:} both variants are scored on the untouched
    hold-out set against the baseline: overall recall, precision,
    \(F_2\), ROC-AUC, and the group recall and false-positive gaps.
  \item
    \textbf{Decision rule (fixed before looking at results):} adopt a
    mitigation only if it narrows the recall gap \textbf{and} keeps
    overall recall at or above \(0.85\) (if the baseline already misses
    \(0.85\), recall must not fall by more than \(0.02\) from the
    baseline); otherwise keep the baseline and report the residual gap
    as a limitation.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

    \textbf{Step 5d takeaways (computed from the results)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Mitigation target:} \texttt{Age\ group}, the attribute with
  the largest baseline recall gap (0.180).
\item
  \textbf{Reweighing (in-processing):} recall gap 0.180 -\textgreater{}
  0.139; overall recall 0.912 -\textgreater{} 0.901; precision 0.685
  -\textgreater{} 0.642.
\item
  \textbf{Group thresholds (post-processing):} recall gap 0.180
  -\textgreater{} 0.074; overall recall 0.912 -\textgreater{} 0.898;
  precision 0.685 -\textgreater{} 0.614.
\item
  \textbf{Decision:} \textbf{Group thresholds (post-processing)}. The
  adopted variant narrows the gap while keeping recall at or above
  0.850.
\item
  \textbf{Trade-off:} fairness interventions move the operating point;
  the precision change above is the extra advisory workload that
  equal-opportunity buys.
\end{itemize}

\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsh{}\PYZsh{}\PYZsh{} Limitations}

\PY{l+s+s2}{1. **Class imbalance:** dropouts are }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{y\PYZus{}train\PYZus{}arr}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{mean}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}\PY{l+s+si}{:}\PY{l+s+s2}{.1\PYZpc{}}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ of the data; weighting raises recall at the cost of precision (extra false alarms).}
\PY{l+s+s2}{2. **Label censoring:** students still *Enrolled* are labelled Retained although their final outcome is unknown.}
\PY{l+s+s2}{3. **Generalisability:** one institution, one country, one cohort period; performance must be re\PYZhy{}validated before use elsewhere.}
\PY{l+s+s2}{4. **Proxies:** excluding gender and marital status does not remove correlated information, so group\PYZhy{}level audits must continue in production.}
\PY{l+s+s2}{5. **Small groups:** subgroups below }\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{MIN\PYZus{}GROUP\PYZus{}N}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{ students are excluded from fairness ratios; their treatment cannot be certified from this data.}
\PY{l+s+s2}{6. **Unobserved causes:** illness, family and work changes are invisible to administrative records.}
\PY{l+s+s2}{7. **Model selection margin:** the top models are close in CV F2, so the choice among them is partly a judgement call.}

\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZsh{}\PYZsh{}\PYZsh{} Recommendations}

\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{} **Operate** the end\PYZhy{}of\PYZhy{}Semester\PYZhy{}2 scoring as advisor decision support, not automated action; every flag triggers a human check\PYZhy{}in.}
\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{} **Monitor** recall, flag rate and the per\PYZhy{}group recall gap each cycle, and retrain when drift or a gap above 0.10 appears.}
\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZhy{} **Route** flagged students to the interventions below, matching the support to the dominant risk signal.}

\PY{l+s+s2}{| Risk profile | Recommended intervention |}
\PY{l+s+s2}{|\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}|\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}\PYZhy{}|}
\PY{l+s+s2}{| High risk and tuition not up to date / debtor | Financial\PYZhy{}aid and emergency\PYZhy{}grant review |}
\PY{l+s+s2}{| High risk and low Semester 1\PYZhy{}2 approval rate | Peer tutoring and academic advising |}
\PY{l+s+s2}{| High risk and mature student (\PYZgt{} 25) | Non\PYZhy{}traditional\PYZhy{}student support and work\PYZhy{}study mentorship |}
\PY{l+s+s2}{| Moderate risk (probability 0.4\PYZhy{}0.6) | Automated check\PYZhy{}in and counselling survey |}
\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}\PY{p}{)}

\hypertarget{limitations}{%
\subsubsection{Limitations}\label{limitations}}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Class imbalance:} dropouts are 32.1\% of the data; weighting
  raises recall at the cost of precision (extra false alarms).
\item
  \textbf{Label censoring:} students still \emph{Enrolled} are labelled
  Retained although their final outcome is unknown.
\item
  \textbf{Generalisability:} one institution, one country, one cohort
  period; performance must be re-validated before use elsewhere.
\item
  \textbf{Proxies:} excluding gender and marital status does not remove
  correlated information, so group-level audits must continue in
  production.
\item
  \textbf{Small groups:} subgroups below 30 students are excluded from
  fairness ratios; their treatment cannot be certified from this data.
\item
  \textbf{Unobserved causes:} illness, family and work changes are
  invisible to administrative records.
\item
  \textbf{Model selection margin:} the top models are close in CV F2, so
  the choice among them is partly a judgement call.
\end{enumerate}

\hypertarget{recommendations}{%
\subsubsection{Recommendations}\label{recommendations}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Operate} the end-of-Semester-2 scoring as advisor decision
  support, not automated action; every flag triggers a human check-in.
\item
  \textbf{Monitor} recall, flag rate and the per-group recall gap each
  cycle, and retrain when drift or a gap above 0.10 appears.
\item
  \textbf{Route} flagged students to the interventions below, matching
  the support to the dominant risk signal.
\end{itemize}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}

\endlastfoot

emergency-grant review \\
High risk and low Semester 1-2 approval rate & Peer tutoring and
academic advising \\
High risk and mature student (\textgreater{} 25) &
Non-traditional-student support and work-study mentorship \\

survey \\

\hypertarget{step-6-final-presentation-communication}{%
\subsection{Step 6: Final Presentation \&
Communication}\label{step-6-final-presentation-communication}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Applied Concept:} Dual-Audience Communication.
\item
  \textbf{Technical deck (this notebook):} the export cell below runs
  \texttt{nbconvert} on the \textbf{saved} notebook and produces an HTML
  report, an interactive slide deck, a LaTeX file and a PDF. A new slide
  starts at each section-header markdown cell, and each slide shows that
  cell's reasoning together with the code and outputs beneath it, so the
  deck reads as one argument: framing, data, features, models,
  explanation, fairness, mitigation, findings, reproducibility. This
  notebook therefore yields 12 slides.
\item
  \textbf{Executive deck (separate PowerPoint):} written for university
  leadership and academic advisors, it translates the measured results
  into business terms: recall and precision as students caught versus
  advisory workload, projected tuition revenue retained against
  intervention cost, the fairness audit and its mitigation, and a phased
  roll-out. Every figure on it is taken from the Step 4 and Step 5
  takeaway blocks, and it targets 12 slides or fewer.
\item
  \textbf{To regenerate the technical deck:} run all cells, save the
  notebook (\texttt{Ctrl+S}) so the file on Drive contains the outputs,
  then run the export cell.
\end{itemize}

            \PY{k}{await} \PY{n}{page}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{goto}\PY{p}{(}\PY{l+s+sa}{f}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{file://}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZob{}}\PY{n}{os}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{path}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{abspath}\PY{p}{(}\PY{n}{html\PYZus{}output\PYZus{}path}\PY{p}{)}\PY{l+s+si}{\PYZcb{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{,} \PY{n}{wait\PYZus{}until}\PY{o}{=}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{l+s+s2}{networkidle}\PY{l+s+s2}{\PYZdq{}}\PY{p}{)}

            \PY{p}{)}
            \PY{k}{await} \PY{n}{browser}\PY{o}{.}\PY{n}{close}\PY{p}{(}\PY{p}{)}

\hypertarget{step-7-github-profile-upload-structure}{%
\subsection{Step 7: GitHub Profile \& Upload
Structure}\label{step-7-github-profile-upload-structure}}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Applied Concept:} Production Project Structuring and
  Reproducible Environments.
\item
  \textbf{How It Works:}

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    \textbf{Folder layout:} the first cell creates \texttt{src/},
    \texttt{notebooks/}, \texttt{data/raw/}, \texttt{data/processed/},
    \texttt{models/} and \texttt{reports/} in the project folder on
    Drive.
  \item
    \textbf{Environment and documentation:} the second cell writes
    \texttt{requirements.txt} (every library the notebook imports) and a
    \texttt{README.md} covering the project overview, repository
    structure and quickstart.
  \item
    \textbf{What gets committed:} this notebook under
    \texttt{notebooks/}, the artefacts written in Step 4
    (\texttt{models/*.joblib}, \texttt{model\_comparison.csv},
    \texttt{best\_model\_config.json}), and the exported decks from Step
    6 under \texttt{reports/}.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}
